If I’m reviewing an audit file, it doesn’t take a lot to pull me off track — a Slack ping, someone talking outside my office, or even just a random thought about marathon training. Then I’ll realize I’ve completely drifted away from what I was supposed to be reviewing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and follow-through causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying, but it has started causing real problems.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and unfinished follow-up on my end have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still for a long review feels physically uncomfortable, like pressure building inside me until I need to stand up, walk to the printer, or invent a reason to move.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even during things that are supposed to be relaxing, like reading at home or sitting quietly before a salsa class starts, I end up humming, checking something, or talking because pure quiet makes me antsy.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On weekends I tell my husband I'm going to rest, but then I bounce from laundry to email to a run to volunteer planning and don't really stop until I'm exhausted.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby makes me tense and impatient, and I keep checking my phone or rehearsing what I need to say instead of just waiting.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I catch myself jumping into conversations before people finish, especially with junior auditors, because the answer or idea feels urgent in my head and I blurt it out before I can stop myself.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my report cards said I was bright but lost assignments, rushed past small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish work. My mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have reached the point where a client review was affected and I worry people are questioning whether I can handle the promotion. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I agreed to because he cannot count on me to complete them reliably.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably interfering for about 1 year and 10 months, since the promotion after the firm-wide award made my workload and responsibilities much harder to juggle.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. But my report cards did say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through small details.\n\nI also remember getting reminders to finish my work, and apparently I talked out of turn sometimes. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Does that count as what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, probably more than I’d like to admit. With family or friends I try to be better about it, but I do catch myself jumping in before someone has finished, especially if I think I know what they’re going to say.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is… I keep starting follow-up things after audits or mentorship events, and I really do mean to finish them. But then a few days later I’ll realize the final email, or a review note, or some form just never actually got done. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m hoping that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus on tasks or easily getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. If I’m reviewing an audit file, it doesn’t take a lot to pull me off track — a Slack ping, someone talking outside my office, or even just a random thought about marathon training. Then I’ll realize I’ve completely drifted away from what I was supposed to be reviewing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and follow-through causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying, but it has started causing real problems.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and unfinished follow-up on my end have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. But my report cards did say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through small details.\n\nI also remember getting reminders to finish my work, and apparently I talked out of turn sometimes. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, but… the part where it really started interfering noticeably has been more recent.\n\nProbably about a year and ten months ago? Around when I got promoted after that firm-wide award. It was a good thing, obviously, and I don’t want to make it sound like I couldn’t handle it, but the workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle. Since then, the focus and restlessness have felt harder to manage, or at least harder to cover up.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… though I try not to. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nIt happens more in conversations at work, especially with junior auditors. If I know where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, it feels almost urgent and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished. Then I realize I’ve cut them off and I feel bad about it.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, I do catch myself blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations or with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, probably more than I’d like to admit. With family or friends I try to be better about it, but I do catch myself jumping in before someone has finished, especially if I think I know what they’re going to say.\n\nIt’s more noticeable at work with junior auditors, honestly. An answer or idea will feel really urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I’ve really stopped myself. I don’t intend it as interrupting, but… yes, I think it comes across that way sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I’d like to say it’s mostly just work stress, but it’s not only work.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten harder to explain away. There was a client review that was affected, and that was… embarrassing. I worry people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I said I would handle, because I haven’t been reliable about finishing them. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t always seen as a big problem. But my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nMy mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it was there pretty early, even if people kind of framed it as me being careless or chatty. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.

At work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.

And at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Since then, the focus and restlessness have felt harder to manage, or at least harder to cover up.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… though I try not to. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nIt happens more in conversations at work, especially with junior auditors. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.

But it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.

I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, but… the part where it really started interfering noticeably has been more recent.\n\nProbably about a year and ten months ago? I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget things I absolutely mean to do, like calling a junior staff member back, paying a bill before the fee hits, or putting a mentorship event on my calendar even after I've agreed to it.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: When I'm sitting in meetings, I'm usually tapping my foot under the table, clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair so much that my colleague has joked she can hear my leg going.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still for a long review feels physically uncomfortable, like pressure building inside me until I need to stand up, walk to the printer, or invent a reason to move.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even during things that are supposed to be relaxing, like reading at home or sitting quietly before a salsa class starts, I end up humming, checking something, or talking because pure quiet makes me antsy.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On weekends I tell my husband I'm going to rest, but then I bounce from laundry to email to a run to volunteer planning and don't really stop until I'm exhausted.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby makes me tense and impatient, and I keep checking my phone or rehearsing what I need to say instead of just waiting.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I catch myself jumping into conversations before people finish, especially with junior auditors, because the answer or idea feels urgent in my head and I blurt it out before I can stop myself.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my report cards said I was bright but lost assignments, rushed past small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish work. My mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have reached the point where a client review was affected and I worry people are questioning whether I can handle the promotion. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I agreed to because he cannot count on me to complete them reliably.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably interfering for about 1 year and 10 months, since the promotion after the firm-wide award made my workload and responsibilities much harder to juggle.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is… I keep starting follow-up things after audits or mentorship events, and I really do mean to finish them. But then a few days later I’ll realize the final email, or a review note, or some form just never actually got done. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m hoping that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus on tasks or easily getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. If I’m reviewing an audit file, it doesn’t take a lot to pull me off track — a Slack ping, someone talking outside my office, or even just a random thought about marathon training. Then I’ll realize I’ve completely drifted away from what I was supposed to be reviewing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and follow-through causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying, but it has started causing real problems.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and unfinished follow-up on my end have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. But my report cards did say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through small details.\n\nI also remember getting reminders to finish my work, and apparently I talked out of turn sometimes. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, but… the part where it really started interfering noticeably has been more recent.\n\nProbably about a year and ten months ago? Around when I got promoted after that firm-wide award. It was a good thing, obviously, and I don’t want to make it sound like I couldn’t handle it, but the workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle. Since then, the focus and restlessness have felt harder to manage, or at least harder to cover up.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… though I try not to. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nIt happens more in conversations at work, especially with junior auditors. If I know where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, it feels almost urgent and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished. Then I realize I’ve cut them off and I feel bad about it.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, I do catch myself blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations or with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, probably more than I’d like to admit. With family or friends I try to be better about it, but I do catch myself jumping in before someone has finished, especially if I think I know what they’re going to say.\n\nIt’s more noticeable at work with junior auditors, honestly. An answer or idea will feel really urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I’ve really stopped myself. I don’t intend it as interrupting, but… yes, I think it comes across that way sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I’d like to say it’s mostly just work stress, but it’s not only work.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten harder to explain away. There was a client review that was affected, and that was… embarrassing. I worry people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I said I would handle, because I haven’t been reliable about finishing them. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t always seen as a big problem. But my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nMy mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it was there pretty early, even if people kind of framed it as me being careless or chatty. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a problem child or anything, because I did well in a lot of ways. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and follow-through causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying, but it has started causing real problems.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and unfinished follow-up on my end have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. But my report cards did say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through small details.\n\nI also remember getting reminders to finish my work, and apparently I talked out of turn sometimes. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, but… the part where it really started interfering noticeably has been more recent.\n\nProbably about a year and ten months ago? Around when I got promoted after that firm-wide award. It was a good thing, obviously, and I don’t want to make it sound like I couldn’t handle it, but the workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle. Since then, the focus and restlessness have felt harder to manage, or at least harder to cover up.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… though I try not to. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nIt happens more in conversations at work, especially with junior auditors. If I know where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, it feels almost urgent and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished. Then I realize I’ve cut them off and I feel bad about it.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, I do catch myself blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations or with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, probably more than I’d like to admit. With family or friends I try to be better about it, but I do catch myself jumping in before someone has finished, especially if I think I know what they’re going to say.\n\nIt’s more noticeable at work with junior auditors, honestly. An answer or idea will feel really urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I’ve really stopped myself. I don’t intend it as interrupting, but… yes, I think it comes across that way sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I’d like to say it’s mostly just work stress, but it’s not only work.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten harder to explain away. There was a client review that was affected, and that was… embarrassing. I worry people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I said I would handle, because I haven’t been reliable about finishing them. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t always seen as a big problem. But my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nMy mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it was there pretty early, even if people kind of framed it as me being careless or chatty. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a problem child or anything, because I did well in a lot of ways. But even back in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to actually finish my work.\n\nMy mother still kind of jokes that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it’s been there in different situations for a long time, even if I’ve tried to explain it away. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had major friendship problems because of it, at least not in a way people have directly told me.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been little things where I’ve seemed distracted or hard to pin down, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. My friends are generally pretty understanding. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’d probably say… moderate? I don’t think it’s severe in the sense that everything is falling apart, and I do still get things done, but it’s more than just a minor annoyance.

At work, the mistakes and the follow-up I don’t finish have started to matter. There was a client review that was affected, and I’ve been worried people are wondering whether I can really handle the promotion. That’s hard to admit.

And at home, my husband has taken over bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, I think moderate is probably the honest answer.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d call it severe, exactly… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

But it has interfered in real ways. At work, the mistakes and things I don’t follow up on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected, and I do worry people are starting to question whether I can handle the promotion.

And at home… my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. That’s embarrassing to say, but it’s probably the clearest example.

So I’d still say moderate rather than severe, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is… I keep starting follow-up things after audits or mentorship events, and I really do mean to finish them. But then a few days later I’ll realize the final email, or a review note, or some form just never actually got done. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m hoping that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus on tasks or easily getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. If I’m reviewing an audit file, it doesn’t take a lot to pull me off track — a Slack ping, someone talking outside my office, or even just a random thought about marathon training. Then I’ll realize I’ve completely drifted away from what I was supposed to be reviewing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and follow-through causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying, but it has started causing real problems.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and unfinished follow-up on my end have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. I’m worried people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion, which is hard to admit because I really do care about doing a good job.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and things I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes, it’s been stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it subtle, especially in meetings, but I’m usually tapping my foot under the table or clicking my pen without really realizing it. Sometimes I shift around in my chair a lot too.\n\nOne colleague has even joked that she can hear my leg going, which was a little embarrassing. So… yes, I guess I am pretty fidgety when I’m trying to sit and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling with focus and fidgetiness at home or in leisure activities, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does show up at home too, though maybe not as obviously.\n\nLike, if I’m trying to read in the evening, I’ll realize I’m humming or picking up my phone to check something instead of just sitting and reading. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and end up chatting or doing something with my hands.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything… I just don’t seem to do “pure quiet” very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or fidgetiness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t like I was failing or anything. But my report cards did say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through small details.\n\nI also remember getting reminders to finish my work, and apparently I talked out of turn sometimes. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack… which is funny, but also kind of embarrassing to admit. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or fidgetiness consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, but… the part where it really started interfering noticeably has been more recent.\n\nProbably about a year and ten months ago? Around when I got promoted after that firm-wide award. It was a good thing, obviously, and I don’t want to make it sound like I couldn’t handle it, but the workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle. Since then, the focus and restlessness have felt harder to manage, or at least harder to cover up.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… though I try not to. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nIt happens more in conversations at work, especially with junior auditors. If I know where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, it feels almost urgent and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished. Then I realize I’ve cut them off and I feel bad about it.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, I do catch myself blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations or with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, probably more than I’d like to admit. With family or friends I try to be better about it, but I do catch myself jumping in before someone has finished, especially if I think I know what they’re going to say.\n\nIt’s more noticeable at work with junior auditors, honestly. An answer or idea will feel really urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I’ve really stopped myself. I don’t intend it as interrupting, but… yes, I think it comes across that way sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I’d like to say it’s mostly just work stress, but it’s not only work.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten harder to explain away. There was a client review that was affected, and that was… embarrassing. I worry people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I said I would handle, because I haven’t been reliable about finishing them. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t always seen as a big problem. But my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nMy mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it was there pretty early, even if people kind of framed it as me being careless or chatty. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a problem child or anything, because I did well in a lot of ways. But even back in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to actually finish my work.\n\nMy mother still kind of jokes that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it’s been there in different situations for a long time, even if I’ve tried to explain it away. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had major friendship problems because of it, at least not in a way people have directly told me.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been little things where I’ve seemed distracted or hard to pin down, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. My friends are generally pretty understanding. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact of your symptoms on your social relationships or friendships outside of work and family?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and family… I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear impact, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have friends, and I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said I’m difficult or anything like that. I’d like to say it’s mostly just work stress, but it’s not only work.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten harder to explain away. There was a client review that was affected, and that was… embarrassing. I worry people are starting to wonder if I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I said I would handle, because I haven’t been reliable about finishing them. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting home too. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these focus or fidgetiness issues when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I did well in school, so it wasn’t always seen as a big problem. But my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nMy mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it was there pretty early, even if people kind of framed it as me being careless or chatty. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, fidgetiness, and impulsivity have caused significant problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, unfortunately. I don’t want to overstate it, because people are mostly kind about it, but it has caused strain.\n\nAt work, some mistakes and follow-up I didn’t finish affected a client review, and that was really hard to face. I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a problem child or anything, because I did well in a lot of ways. But even back in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to actually finish my work.\n\nMy mother still kind of jokes that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it’s been there in different situations for a long time, even if I’ve tried to explain it away. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had major friendship problems because of it, at least not in a way people have directly told me.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been little things where I’ve seemed distracted or hard to pin down, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. My friends are generally pretty understanding. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact of your symptoms on your social relationships or friendships outside of work and family?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and family… I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear impact, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have friends, and I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said I’m difficult or anything like that. Maybe there are small things I don’t always pick up on, but I wouldn’t want to assume. Socially, I think I do okay overall.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your symptoms as mild, moderate, or severe in terms of how much they interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say… moderate? I don’t think it’s severe in the sense that everything is falling apart, and I do still get things done, but it’s more than just a minor annoyance.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the follow-up I don’t finish have started to matter. There was a client review that was affected, and I’ve been worried people are wondering whether I can really handle the promotion. That’s hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has taken over bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, I think moderate is probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity interfered with your ability to perform daily tasks to the point where you would describe them as severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it severe, exactly… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it has interfered in real ways. At work, the mistakes and things I don’t follow up on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected, and I do worry people are starting to question whether I can handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably.   },

I’ve been worried since then that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over some bills and commitments I had agreed to handle, because I haven’t been consistent enough. He doesn’t say it cruelly, but I know it frustrates him that he can’t always count on me to finish what I said I would. So yes, I guess it has affected relationships, especially trust. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where your focus, fidgetiness, or impulsivity issues seemed to significantly improve or disappear for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really disappear, no. I mean, there have been times when I could sort of compensate better, so it looked more under control.\n\nBut it’s been noticeably interfering for… about a year and ten months, I think. Since the promotion after the firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got much harder to juggle. I kept thinking I just needed to get organized and adjust, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months during your life where you did not have any significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been a full six-month stretch where it was just… gone, no.\n\nI can think of times where I managed it better or it didn’t stand out as much, but the period where it’s really been noticeably interfering has been about a year and ten months now — since the promotion after that firm-wide award, when my workload and responsibilities got a lot harder to juggle.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it disappeared for six months. Is that answering it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity consistently across different settings and situations for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a problem child or anything, because I did well in a lot of ways. But even back in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to actually finish my work.\n\nMy mother still kind of jokes that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting in my backpack. So… yes, I guess it’s been there in different situations for a long time, even if I’ve tried to explain it away. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had major friendship problems because of it, at least not in a way people have directly told me.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been little things where I’ve seemed distracted or hard to pin down, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. My friends are generally pretty understanding. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact of your symptoms on your social relationships or friendships outside of work and family?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and family… I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear impact, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have friends, and I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said I’m difficult or anything like that. Maybe there are small things I don’t always pick up on, but I wouldn’t want to assume. Socially, I think I do okay overall.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your symptoms as mild, moderate, or severe in terms of how much they interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say… moderate? I don’t think it’s severe in the sense that everything is falling apart, and I do still get things done, but it’s more than just a minor annoyance.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the follow-up I don’t finish have started to matter. There was a client review that was affected, and I’ve been worried people are wondering whether I can really handle the promotion. That’s hard to admit.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has taken over bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, I think moderate is probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity interfered with your ability to perform daily tasks to the point where you would describe them as severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it severe, exactly… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it has interfered in real ways. At work, the mistakes and things I don’t follow up on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected, and I do worry people are starting to question whether I can handle the promotion.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. That’s embarrassing to say, but it’s probably the clearest example.\n\nSo I’d still say moderate rather than severe, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms were so severe that they significantly impacted your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “so severe,” because that feels a little dramatic, and I am still managing… mostly.\n\nBut yes, it has definitely impacted daily life and my relationship at home. At work, the mistakes I make and the follow-up I don’t finish have gotten serious enough that a client review was affected. And I’ve been worried people are questioning whether I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over bills and commitments that I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the part that makes it feel more significant.\n\nSo… not severe in my mind, maybe, but it has had a real impact.
